### [Full automation of AI R&D probably yields a large speed up even without a software-only singularity](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jfwhvd43sbpkGTLyn/full-automation-of-ai-r-and-d-probably-yields-a-large-speed)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><em>This is a somewhat technical note.</em></p>
<p>By "software-only singularity", I mean that, after full automation of AI R&amp;D, progress gets faster and faster due to smarter AIs driving increasingly fast rates of improvement in algorithms (overcoming diminishing returns), and that this lasts long enough to yield a large amount of progress (e.g. at least 4 years of progress in 1 year). The equivalent statement in jargon is: r is significantly greater than 1 (implying progress is getting faster and faster) and this remains the case for long enough to get large amounts of progress. For context, see <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/how-quick-and-big-would-a-software-intelligence-explosion-be">How quick and big would a software intelligence explosion be?</a></p>
<p>Even without a "software-only singularity", I think full automation of AI R&amp;D probably greatly speeds up progress for two main reasons:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>You get a one-time speed up from automation and this speed up seems like it will be pretty large</strong> (even with r&lt;1). See <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/how-quick-and-big-would-a-software-intelligence-explosion-be">How quick and big would a software intelligence explosion be?</a> for discussion and see the <a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/">AI Futures Model</a> for an end-to-end model that naturally incorporates this effect. Quantitatively, with my median parameters but r=0.7, <a href="https://accelerated-ai-progress.streamlit.app/">the model</a> from <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/how-quick-and-big-would-a-software-intelligence-explosion-be">How quick and big would a software…</a> indicates you get 3.5 years of progress in the first year after full automation of AI R&amp;D while assuming you aren't scaling up compute at all in this period. This is a huge amount of progress! To be clear, this is somewhat more progress than I actually expect in this situation conditional on this value for r, but it's still relevant evidence about the size of this effect.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span></li>
<li><strong>Even after the one-time speed up, increasing the available quantity of compute now has larger returns than it did when humans were the core source of AI R&amp;D labor.</strong> When humans were the bottlenecking source of AI R&amp;D labor, increases in compute let you run more/bigger experiments and train larger AIs. After AIs have fully automated AI R&amp;D, additional compute can still be used for experiments and training, but it also yields improvements to the AI labor force doing AI R&amp;D (making them smarter/faster/cheaper-to-run), which means all the compute will now be better utilized. Also, additional compute can be used to run more of these AI laborers (and potentially run them considerably faster depending on chip improvements). This is a feedback loop: these better AIs do better experiments that yield better AIs and so on. Even if this software-only feedback loop is subcritical (which we're conditioning on in a "no software-only singularity" scenario), it still means every increase in compute will now drive more progress. I haven't yet found a nice and clean way to model this in isolation, but I suspect this effect is large, perhaps doubling, tripling or even quadrupling the rate of progress you would have otherwise seen (without AIs automating AI R&amp;D) given some rate of compute increase.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span> That is, until you get sufficiently close to algorithmic limits that the returns curve looks substantially less favorable. (This will depend on r. If r is close to 1, the feedback loop is almost critical, so a small proportional increase in compute drives a huge amount of additional progress. But even if r is only 0.5, I currently tentatively expect this feedback loop makes progress a bit more than 2x faster assuming my default guesses at some other parameters.)</li>
</ul>
<p>We can also analyze this by looking at <a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/p?base=eli-04-02-26&amp;arts=1.7864875748520508">an example trajectory in the AI Futures Model that barely misses a software-only singularity</a> and seeing how fast progress is after full automation of AI R&amp;D. This trajectory involves a little over 2 years of progress in the year after full automation of AI R&amp;D (SAR). This corresponds to going from full automation of AI R&amp;D (SAR) to <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LjgcRbptarrRfJWtR/a-breakdown-of-ai-capability-levels-focused-on-ai-r-and-d">Top-human-Expert-Dominating AI (TEDAI)</a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span> in a bit less than a year, which is a lot of progress. (Quantitatively, it involves going from a 24x AI R&amp;D software acceleration to a 270x AI R&amp;D software acceleration in a year.) I suspect the AI Futures Model modestly underestimates takeoff speeds and one-time acceleration effects due to effectively acting as though AI speed and quantity don't matter outside of coding automation.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>There are other (indirect) reasons AI progress might speed up around when AIs automate AI R&amp;D:</p>
<ul>
<li>This level of AI capability might drive above-recent-trend investment and revenue that allows for buying more compute.</li>
<li>If one company pulls significantly ahead (and especially if it had fully automated AI R&amp;D<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-5">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span>), that company might be able to more easily get the compute of other trailing companies (by <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/higher-limits-spacex">buying it from the trailing company</a> or by waiting for those companies to collapse)<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-6">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-6">[6]</a></sup>
    </span>.</li>
<li>AIs might speed up hardware R&amp;D (developing better chip designs, accelerating fab research, building more fabs faster) around this point.</li>
</ul>
<p>One important caveat is that by the time AIs automate AI R&amp;D, the rate of compute scaling may be substantially lower than it is today. Thus, the default/trend rate of AI progress may be lower, so the corresponding acceleration would be relative to a lower baseline. This is directly applicable for the "further compute has increased returns" argument and maybe has a modest effect on the size of the one-time speed up (the size of the one-time speed up is sensitive to how much returns from further labor effort have diminished at a given level of compute).</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-1"><p>If I remember correctly, this model effectively acts as though you go from no automation acceleration directly to full automation, while in practice earlier AIs will substantially accelerate AI R&amp;D, meaning that returns to effort will already have substantially diminished by the point you reach full automation. As in, full automation will be a large acceleration relative to a human-only baseline, but a relatively smaller acceleration relative to AIs that existed 6 months before full automation, so much of the low-hanging fruit will already be plucked. You can model this in an ad hoc way by reducing the initial speed-up parameter such that it corresponds to the speed-up over AIs that existed 8 months prior to full automation; with my parameter guesses, this yields around 2.5 years of progress in the first year. (There is a gradual boost setting that smooths out the automation returns over a longer period, but I think this period is unrealistically long such that you don't see one-time speed-up effects.) <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-2"><p>Historically, progress has been driven by both scaling up compute and scaling up labor. However, I expect scaling up labor has been a small fraction of the effect in recent years. Compute for algorithms and training has been scaled up by around 4x per year while company employee count has 3x'd each year. But employee count 3x'ing is way worse than making all employees operate 3x faster due to a diminishing labor pool, (mostly one-time) onboarding costs, and parallelization penalties (while 4x more compute at current margins is pretty close to as good as getting compute that's 4x serially faster). I think the discount from a diminishing labor pool and from onboarding makes the 3x increase in the number of employees roughly as good as a "free" 2x increase in employee count at equal quality. Then, the parallelization penalty further reduces this 2x increase to being as valuable as having existing employees operate ~1.3x faster. Thus, I expect the labor increase is much less important than a 4x increase in compute. So it's fair to model the large majority of recent progress as being driven by increases in compute, where the value mostly comes from being able to run more experiments. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-3"><p>TEDAI: AIs which strictly dominate top human experts in virtually all cognitive tasks (i.e., doable via remote work). <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-4"><p>This is in part because it doesn't model shifting to research directions that are more effective in the low-compute but plentiful-labor regime. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-5"><p>Fully automated AI R&amp;D makes moderate advantages more likely to be stable/predictable because now the labor part of AI R&amp;D is likely commoditized and similar between companies (reducing variance). However, maintaining a lead ultimately requires maintaining a compute advantage (a large software lead can probably be converted into a compute advantage): if a trailing company had more compute and was able to hold on to a compute advantage (despite the potentially decisive advantages of the leading company), we should expect them to eventually catch up and overtake because labor is commoditized after full automation. I suspect it will be hard for significantly trailing companies to maintain a compute advantage if the leading company pulls far ahead on software due to speed ups from AI R&amp;D. In the most extreme case, the leading company (or the AIs of the leading company) might literally take over the world, neutralizing prior compute advantages of trailing companies. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-6"><p>Investors might be incentivized to pressure the trailing company to sell their compute to the leading company even if the leadership of the company isn't inclined to do this. Investors have limited power so this isn't clearly sufficient, but a deal could be designed to give the leadership of the trailing company additional power or possibly financial upside, so that they are incentivized to sell. Also, the leading company might just end up being extremely powerful, in the limit literally fully taking over the world. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9AswB5kjg3TtsLtpT-6">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jfwhvd43sbpkGTLyn/full-automation-of-ai-r-and-d-probably-yields-a-large-speed#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The ballad of TIGIT](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iyatrrJaHysuhrqXe/the-ballad-of-tigit)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iyatrrJaHysuhrqXe/nbq5urlsxafzkh8kkgbp" /><p><span>There exist drug classes that seem, in retrospect, cursed. As these chemicals worm their way through the clinical trial system, they consume billions of dollars along the way, and squelch through thousands of sick patients. When finally it dawns on everyone how useless the whole endeavour was, the drugs life is at last cut short, nothing useful left in its destructive wake. The prototype here are </span><a href="https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD016297" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>amyloid-beta drugs</span></a><span>. These are Alzheimer’s treatments that are widely perceived as immense disappointments, </span><a href="https://www.cochrane.org/about-us/news/anti-amyloid-alzheimers-drugs-show-no-clinically-meaningful-effect" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>with the negative sentiment even leaking to the broader public</span></a><span>. To be fair to these chemicals, </span><a href="https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2212948" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>the story here is a bit more complicated</span></a><span> than the tabloids let on. Lots of amyloid research was</span><i><span> not</span></i><span> fake, and </span><a href="https://www.fda.gov/news-events/press-announcements/fda-converts-novel-alzheimers-disease-treatment-traditional-approval" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>the drugs may genuinely be useful for early-stage Alzheimer’s</span></a><span>. But they remain, regardless, disappointments.</span></p><p><span>Beyond amyloid-beta, which has been steadily disappointing for awhile now, there is one other such category of drug whose particular dance has just recently wrapped up. It may very well someday gets its chance in the spotlight, but it will take time. Because it—just like every other chemical in this class—shares a searing, burning radioactivity. You should not touch them. You should not suggest touching them. In fact, no serious person should touch them for years to come, because to do so will be to receive the scorn of other serious people.</span></p><p><span>What I am talking about are, of course, TIGIT drugs.</span></p><p><span>TIGIT emerged in the wake of boundless enthusiasm from </span><a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/immunology/articles/10.3389/fimmu.2019.02965/full" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>over a century of grueling cancer immunotherapy research</span></a><span>. Much of this work went nowhere, but a small fragment of it helped produce the </span><a href="https://www.thebureauinvestigates.com/stories/2026-04-13/big-pharmas-biggest-seller-how-one-drug-makes-more-money-than-mcdonalds" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>most valuable molecule in existence</span></a><span>: Keytruda (pembrolizumab). This drug was so astonishingly, grossly successful that it would be barely an exaggeration to credit Keytruda with </span><i><span>creating</span></i><span> a Big Pharma. Since its approval in 2014, it has saved millions of years’ worth of patient lives, and will likely continue to save millions more.</span></p><p><span>So, if you worked in pharma R&amp;D in the mid-2010’s, and you were on the hunt for the next big thing, “</span><i><span>something like Keytruda</span></i><span>” was the most attractive thing on the board. And TIGIT drugs were supposed to be that.</span></p><p><span>An explanation of what TIGIT actually is would require you to hold roughly seven concepts in your mind at the same time, the names of which—in characteristic immunology fashion—are not helpful in the slightest. What is important to understand is that TIGIT is a particular protein, and theorized to be </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10249258/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>another immune-system brake</span></a><span>. The aforementioned century of immunology research had already proven that these brakes mattered: Keytruda worked by blocking a different brake and allowing immune cells to attack tumors again. TIGIT seemed to offer the same promise, since tumors appeared to exploit it to quiet nearby immune cells. But this story was set to be even more intriguing. Unlike Keytruda’s target, TIGIT sat at an especially </span><a href="https://jitc.bmj.com/content/10/4/e004711" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>busy intersection of immune regulation</span></a><span>. Blocking it might not merely release one brake, but rather two brakes </span><i><span>and</span></i><span> one accelerator, tilting the local immune environment towards such an absurdly anticancer direction that it was unthinkable that it wouldn’t be clinically effective.</span></p><p><span>So, the theory went: block TIGIT, or create an ‘anti-TIGIT’ drug, and you’ve got something even better than Keytruda on your hands.</span></p><p><span>Dollar signs appeared in the eyes of nearly every pharmaceutical executive upon learning this. Roche was the first here, their group establishing the above scientific observations, publishing them in a 2014 paper. "</span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25465800/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>The immunoreceptor TIGIT regulates antitumor and antiviral CD8+ T cell effector function</span></a><span>”. The molecule that emerged from this work was something called tiragolumab; the first anti-TIGIT drug to exist.</span></p><p><span>Its initial clinical debut was at ASCO 2020, a major oncology conference. There, Roche discussed the results of a </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35576957/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>135-patient phase 2 trial</span></a><span> in metastatic non-small-cell lung cancer, randomizing patients one-to-one to the standard-of-care, plus either tiragolumab or placebo. The combination produced a </span><a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT03563716" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>response rate of 31% versus 16%</span></a><span> in the placebo. Tiragolumab seemed to work. Yes, it wasn’t a cure for cancer, but neither was Keytruda, which still managed to rake in </span><a href="https://www.merck.com/news/merck-announces-fourth-quarter-and-full-year-2024-financial-results/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>nearly ten billion dollars a year</span></a><span>. The FDA granted tiragolumab a </span><a href="https://www.roche.com/media/releases/med-cor-2021-01-05" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>breakthrough designation in January 2021</span></a><span> on the basis of that study, and, within a year, Roche began to spin up phase 3 trials.</span></p><p><span>Blood was in the water for TIGIT, and though Roche was first to bite, others followed. Merck had vibostolimab. BMS had </span><a href="https://www.oncologypipeline.com/apexonco/bristol-seeks-novelty-tigit" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>BMS-986207</span></a><span>. BeiGene had ociperlimab, for which </span><a href="https://www.novartis.com/news/media-releases/novartis-strengthens-immunotherapy-pipeline-option-collaboration-and-license-agreement-beigene-tigit-inhibitor-ociperlimab" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Novartis paid $300 million in early 2021 for co-development rights</span></a><span>. Arcus had domvanalimab, for which Gilead in late 2020 </span><a href="https://www.biospace.com/gilead-goes-all-in-on-revised-arcus-option-deal-totaling-725-million" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>paid $175 million up front plus a $200 million equity position in the company</span></a><span>. iTeos, a little Belgian immuno-oncology outfit, had something called EOS-448, which </span><a href="https://www.gsk.com/en-gb/media/press-releases/gsk-and-iteos-therapeutics-announce-development/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>GSK licensed in mid-2021 for $625 million upfront</span></a><span>. Everyone wanted a bite and was willing to pay for it.</span></p><p><span>Typically, with drug classes that have as much buzz as TIGIT did, companies like to run multiple trials in parallel, each one focused on a different cancer or patient subpopulation. This is to avoid a situation where your drug works spectacularly, it gets approved for the cancer subtype you tested it on, and then you have to watch on as your competitors’ drugs flood the remaining subtypes with their copycat chemicals. And the theoretical evidence for TIGIT was so strong, so overwhelming, that when combined with the promising phase 2 results, pushed Roche to go all in. At one point, they were running </span><b><span>twelve concurrent Phase 2 and Phase 3 trials</span></b><span>, each focused on a slightly different patient population, altogether covering ~5,000 human lives. This effort, which was branded ‘SKYSCRAPER’, represented one of the largest parallel-indication programs in modern immuno-oncology, its total costs likely running into the multiple billions.</span></p><p><span>In May 2022, the first crack showed. Roche reported that </span><a href="https://www.roche.com/media/releases/med-cor-2022-03-30" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>its first major Phase 3 trial, in first-line small-cell lung cancer (SKYSCRAPER-02), had missed on progression-free survival</span></a><span>, or PFS. But this was not the end of the world. Small-cell lung cancer is a rather miserable disease. Relatively little works here anyway. This subtype has swallowed a long procession of drugs that excelled in other settings, so this was not viewed so much as a failure as it was an admirable, Hail Mary attempt that was almost assuredly not going to work out anyway.</span></p><p><span>But a few weeks later came a bigger problem: </span><a href="https://www.roche.com/media/releases/med-cor-2022-05-11" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Roche’s flagship lung-cancer trial (SKYSCRAPER-01), tested on an ostensibly curable type of lung cancer,</span></a><span> also missed on PFS. To be clear: they did not miss it by a lot. Roche would spend the next two years insisting that the values were in the right direction, just not at statistical significance.</span></p><p><span>Either way, the company demurred, the PFS metric is not what matters most. They were not wrong. PFS means something quite specific: from the start of the trial, how long did it take for a patient’s cancer to either grow on imaging or kill them. It is a useful data point, but it is ultimately a fuzzy surrogate of the metric that people </span><i><span>actually</span></i><span> care about: </span><a href="https://www.fda.gov/media/71195/download" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>overall survival, or OS</span></a><span>. How long did this patient live? Unfortunately, this metric takes years to read out and is confounded by whatever subsequent lines of therapy the patient picks up after the trial, so PFS is an often relied-on proxy metric.</span></p><p><span>And Roche believed that OS would ultimately exonerate tiragolumab.</span></p><p><span>And </span><a href="https://www.reuters.com/business/healthcare-pharmaceuticals/roche-reports-inadvertent-disclosure-lung-cancer-immunotherapy-study-2023-08-23/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>in August 2023</span></a><span>, Roche ‘accidentally’ leaked data suggesting that overall survival had indeed improved on the drug. </span><a href="https://www.fiercebiotech.com/biotech/roche-posts-interim-tigit-overall-survival-data-after-inadvertent-disclosure-sending-stocks" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>The stock ticked up</span></a><span> on what in retrospect was the last uncomplicated moment of optimism in the TIGIT race.</span></p><p><span>On November 26, 2024, Roche reported the final OS analysis. </span><b><span>The flagship trial had missed</span></b><span>. The survival trend had narrowed to the point of insignificance, and the trial that was supposed to anchor the entire program—the indication on which Breakthrough Therapy Designation had been granted, the signal on which ten other trials had been launched—could no longer be the anchor.</span></p><p><span>But the flagship’s collapse wasn’t even the nadir. The nadir, really, was </span><a href="https://www.roche.com/media/releases/med-cor-2024-07-04" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Roche’s worse-than-nothing readout in July 2024 (SKYSCRAPER-06)</span></a><span>, in the interim between the flagship’s PFS miss and its OS miss. It did not merely fail to show superiority to the standard of care, but was actively </span><i><span>worse</span></i><span>. </span><b><span>Patients on tiragolumab died faster than the control group.</span></b></p><p><a href="https://www.oncologypipeline.com/apexonco/another-roche-tigit-disappointment" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Everything began to unwind</span></a><span> from here on out for Roche. A planned follow-up was canceled before it had really begun, and another was deprioritized. Over the subsequent year, the GI indications collapsed one after another, a locally advanced esophageal-cancer trial failed, a head-and-neck study was abandoned, and </span><a href="https://www.oncologypipeline.com/apexonco/5000-patients-later-roche-scraps-its-tigit?utm_source=chatgpt.com" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>the last major Roche hope, in first-line liver cancer</span></a><span>, missed PFS with no trend toward OS. The only success, awkwardly, was a trial in esophageal squamous-cell carcinoma (SKYSCRAPER-08), which produced statistically significant survival results. But by the time the</span><a href="https://ascopost.com/issues/march-25-2024/esophageal-squamous-cell-cancer-overall-survival-improved-with-tiragolumab-and-atezolizumab-plus-chemotherapy/?utm_source=chatgpt.com" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span> full paper appeared </span></a><span>in early 2026, Roche had already removed tiragolumab from its pipeline.</span></p><p><span>The TIGIT game, for Roche, had ended.</span></p><p><span>But what of the other players? Could it be that tiragolumab was the problem, and not the TIGIT hypothesis? Perhaps a different molecule, one still targeting TIGIT, would have worked.</span></p><p><span>After Roche, Merck was the second biggest believer in TIGIT. Remember when I said Keytruda had almost single-handedly created a Big Pharma? Merck </span><i><span>is</span></i><span> that pharma. And with their patent over Keytruda set to expire in 2028, they were the ones most interested—and best positioned—to own its successor. In their exuberance, they decided to match Roche: twelve parallel trials of their own, each one running an anti-TIGIT drug called vibostolimab.</span></p><p><span>The same pattern repeated. In May 2023, </span><a href="https://www.merck.com/news/merck-provides-update-on-phase-3-keyvibe-010-trial-evaluating-an-investigational-coformulation-of-vibostolimab-and-pembrolizumab-as-adjuvant-treatment-for-patients-with-resected-high-risk-melanoma/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Merck’s melanoma trial was halted</span></a><span> because vibostolimab was causing such a high rate of immune-related adverse events that patients were discontinuing therapy faster than any efficacy signal could accumulate. In August 2024, </span><a href="https://www.merck.com/news/merck-provides-update-on-phase-3-keyvibe-008-trial-evaluating-an-investigational-fixed-dose-combination-of-vibostolimab-and-pembrolizumab-in-patients-with-extensive-stage-small-cell-lung-cancer/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>a small-cell lung-cancer trial was halted</span></a><span> for OS futility, with the combination arm running </span><i><span>worse</span></i><span> than the control on both efficacy and safety. In December 2024, </span><a href="https://www.merck.com/news/merck-provides-update-on-keyvibe-and-keyform-clinical-development-programs-evaluating-investigational-vibostolimab-and-favezelimab-fixed-dose-combinations-with-pembrolizumab/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>two more lung-cancer studies were abandoned</span></a><span> halfway through the trial. And by 2025, Merck </span><a href="https://oncodaily.com/science/merck-208151" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>announced the discontinuation</span></a><span> of the entire vibostolimab program.</span></p><p><span>But there was one last hope. What if the biological story here wasn’t complete? What if the </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25465800/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>original Roche paper</span></a><span>, a decade back at this point, had gotten something wrong?</span></p><p><span>Every anti-TIGIT drug was structured like an antibody, a protein shaped like a ‘Y’. Only the top two segments—known as the Fab region—are actually interacting with TIGIT, while the bottom region—known as the ‘Fc’ region—interacts with an entirely separate set of receptors on an entirely separate set of immune cells. Typically, the two work in tandem. The Fab region binds to TIGIT-expressing cells, and the Fc region grabs onto nearby immune cells, forcing them to kill whatever the Fab region has attached to; a phenomenon called </span><a href="https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00262-025-04128-7" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC)</span></a><span>. Importantly, TIGIT is expressed on tumor cells and immune-suppressing cells, so ADCC was a reasonable thing to aim for.</span></p><p><span>But this could backfire. TIGIT was also expressed on the cancer-fighting T-cells that the drug is meant to support. So yes, these drugs may kill your enemies, but they will also kill your army, and the empirical net effect of this is little impact on how long a cancer patient will live. But it doesn’t need to be this way. While naturally-created antibodies </span><i><span>always</span></i><span> perform ADCC to some varying degree, there’s a lot more room for creativity with antibodies created in a vat: you can simply break the Fc region by mutating it. The result is </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38635895/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>‘Fc-silent’ antibodies</span></a><span>, which should still </span><i><span>bind</span></i><span> to TIGIT-expressing cells, but not kill them. Whether this would work at all was, luckily, testable. While Roche and Merck had spent billions on their Fc-active molecules, Arcus and Gilead had, in parallel, been developing </span><a href="https://arcusbio.com/our-science/clinical-candidates/domvanalimab/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>domvanalimab: an Fc-silent anti-TIGIT antibody</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>For most of 2024 and 2025, domvanalimab was carrying the collective hope of the entire TIGIT field on its shoulders, as essentially the last well-powered phase 3 program still running with a mechanistically distinct molecule. Starting in early 2024, the drug entered the crucial test of the Fc-silent hypothesis: a phase 3 trial in upper-GI cancers (STAR-221).</span></p><p><span>It did not work. In December 2025, </span><a href="https://www.gilead.com/company/company-statements/2025/gilead-provides-update-on-phase-3-star-221-study" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>the trial was halted</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>And what of everyone else? The smaller bets around the edges were erased with even less ceremony. Novartis had paid $300 million in December 2021 for an option on BeiGene's ociperlimab, and in July 2023, Novartis looked at the emerging TIGIT phase 2 data across the field and </span><a href="https://www.fiercebiotech.com/biotech/novartis-retreats-tigit-handing-300m-candidate-back-beigene" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>simply handed the rights back</span></a><span>, forfeiting the option fee in what turned out to be one of the better decisions any business-development team made that year. BeiGene continued on alone, and in April 2025 </span><a href="https://www.fiercebiotech.com/biotech/beigene-abandons-ociperlimab-over-poor-phase-3-prospects-latest-blow-tigits" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>its phase 3 trial was terminated for futility on an interim OS analysis</span></a><span>, ending the program. GSK's bet was the most expensive and, in some sense, the most depressing. In June 2021 they had paid iTeos, a Belgian immuno-oncology shop, $625 million upfront plus up to $1.45 billion in milestones for belrestotug. Again, zero benefit. </span><a href="https://www.gsk.com/en-gb/media/press-releases/gsk-provides-update-on-belrestotug-development-programme/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>GSK and iTeos mutually terminated the program</span></a><span>, the collaboration, and any further enrollment in the study, all in the same press release. Two weeks later, </span><a href="https://www.reuters.com/business/healthcare-pharmaceuticals/iteos-plans-shut-down-operations-after-cancer-therapy-setback-2025-05-28/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>iTeos announced that it was winding down</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://www.fiercebiotech.com/biotech/iteos-reeling-tigt-fail-becomes-latest-prize-deal-hungry-concentra" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>was later bought by an outfit known for acquiring down-on-their-luck biotechs</span></a><span> in hopes of selling off their parts.</span></p><p><span>Despite it all, TIGIT has not yet technically died. As one article puts it: ‘</span><a href="https://www.oncologypipeline.com/apexonco/astrazeneca-becomes-tigits-last-man-standing" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>AstraZeneca becomes TIGIT’s last man standing</span></a><span>’. Their drug is called rilvegostomig and is currently in </span><i><span>eleven</span></i><span> Phase 3 trials. Unfortunately, the core thesis of the drug is contingent on Fc-silence meaning anything, so it is difficult to imagine history pans out differently here.</span></p><p><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12911736/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>In 2026, a BMJ Oncology analysis </span></a><span>would give a clinical name to what had happened: “</span><i><span>herding</span></i><span>.” The authors estimated that nearly 49,000 patients had been enrolled in anti-TIGIT trials by pharmaceutical companies, at a cost of more than $3 billion, all because their fellow pharmaceutical companies were doing the same thing. The Fc-silent hypothesis had been tested and had failed. The Fc-active hypothesis had been tested and had failed. Combinations with every conceivable drug, across every conceivable demographic, across every conceivable cancer diagnosis—all of it, tested, and all of it, in the aggregate, failed.</span></p><p><span>Today, amongst many oncology investors and researchers, TIGIT has become close to a dirty word. Never, ever suggest touching TIGIT. It will not work.</span></p><p><span>After all this, one cannot help but ask: </span><i><span>what had gone so wrong?</span></i></p><p><span>Unfortunately, the field does not yet have a clear answer. And it is unlikely there is </span><i><span>one </span></i><span>answer. As is often the case in biology, a target that sits at the busy intersection of many valuable things is that the very thing that makes it attractive as a target also makes it almost impossible to reason about cleanly. Perhaps TIGIT alone does not move the immune system in one direction, but instead tugs on a dense, locally contingent web of signals whose meaning changed from tumor to tumor, patient to patient. Perhaps modulating TIGIT is genuinely important, but would require the modulation of a half-dozen other targets for it to have the benefit that everyone expected of it. Perhaps TIGIT was actually transformative, but only for a very specific cohort of patient that the clinical trial apparatus is simply not built to discover at scale. Perhaps something else entirely.</span></p><p><span>What does feel likely is that TIGIT was </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> nonsense. The billions wasted was not an outgrowth of the publish-or-perish industrial complex, or something of the like. Genuinely intelligent theory was here, backed by years of genuinely intelligent wet-lab effort, and its eventual failure was, as far as I can tell, predicted by absolutely nobody. In fact, TIGIT was the golden child of what translational biology </span><i><span>ought</span></i><span> to look like. It had human genetics-adjacent plausibility, clean immunology, druggable extracellular geometry, a commercial precedent, and early clinical signal. It simply did not work.</span></p><p><span>Lots of people boil down the problem of drug discovery to toxicology, or target selection, or trial scalability. All these matter, yes. But sometimes the people behind a drug can do everything right, and it will still fail. Keytruda taught the pharmaceutical industry that the immune system had brakes, and it earned a place in the annals of cancer biology history for that. TIGIT taught the more humiliating, expensive lesson: not every brake is attached to wheels.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iyatrrJaHysuhrqXe/the-ballad-of-tigit#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Announcing Geodesic Research](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xBbYGer8w45kxkaWr/announcing-geodesic-research)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://geodesicresearch.ai/" rel="noreferrer"><span>We</span></a><span>'re a Cambridge, UK-based AI safety organisation that’s asking: </span><i><span>how can we build the most robust alignment initialisations for capable LLMs</span></i><span>?</span></p><p><span>We’re one of the few non-profit organisations positioned to answer this question empirically. We have the engineering experience, and now the compute, to conduct data intensive interventions across the model training pipeline. This post lays out our research agenda and theory of change, and what we are looking for in technical hires.</span><a href="https://airtable.com/appuugUGFPJEy6XCX/pagVvTAIa81UxrEir/form" rel="noreferrer"><span> </span><b><span>Applications are open here</span></b></a><span>.</span></p><h2><span>Research agenda</span></h2><p><span>TLDR: Long-horizon capabilities RL may be the most critical source of misalignment. Misalignment instilled during capabilities RL may be difficult to remove afterwards. Geodesic Research’s mission is to develop the science of providing robustly-aligned initialisations for RL, where alignment priors persist through the remainder of training.</span></p><p><span>Our</span><a href="https://alignmentpretraining.ai/"><span> </span></a><a href="https://alignmentpretraining.ai/"><span>seminal work on alignment pretraining</span></a><span> showed that you can bake alignment priors into base models. Frontier labs are now using these techniques in production: for example, Anthropic's </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/teaching-claude-why"><span>recent</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/msm/"><span>work</span></a><span> heavily leans on improving alignment priors. But it’s clear that, in the face of production post-training, alignment pretraining is </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/how-far-does-alignment-midtraining-generalize/"><span>not a one-size-fits-all solution</span></a><span>. So now, we are framing pre- and midtraining interventions within the rest of the model training stack.</span></p><p><span>The evidence points towards extended reinforcement learning being a likely cause of alignment failures at the frontier. RL is liable to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span>select for</span></a><span> undesired cognitive and behavioural habits, such as </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/metagaming/"><span>metagaming</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/expanding-on-sycophancy/"><span>sycophancy</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WewsByywWNhX9rtwi/current-ais-seem-pretty-misaligned-to-me"><span>apparent-success seeking</span></a><span>, or </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/08ab9158070959f88f296514c21b7facce6f52bc.pdf"><span>taking unsanctioned actions to complete tasks</span></a><span>.  Models that learn these behaviours may also become </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18397"><span>broadly misaligned</span></a><span>. In fact, these degradations have already been noted in </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/how-far-does-alignment-midtraining-generalize/"><span>replications of alignment pretraining</span></a><span>, and Evan Hubinger lists this as one of the core reasons </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/epjuxGnSPof3GnMSL/alignment-remains-a-hard-unsolved-problem"><span>alignment remains a hard and unsolved problem</span></a><span>. Apollo Research's</span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/blog/apollo-update-may-2026/"><span> </span></a><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/blog/apollo-update-may-2026/"><span>recent update</span></a><span> makes a similar diagnosis; they are now studying whether misalignment scales unfavourably with RL. Once a model has learned misaligned behaviours or goals, they may be </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25891"><span>difficult to remove</span></a><span> with subsequent training, and more advanced models may be able to </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14093"><span>guard them from removal</span></a><span>. For this reason, we believe it’s important to avoid their formation in the first place.</span></p><p><span>Our current research focus is on building a </span><b><span>robust initialisation for alignment.  </span></b><span>What we believe the field has </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> yet seriously tested</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhblzai0p5z"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhblzai0p5z">[1]</a></sup></span><span> is the size of the lever that comes before capabilities RL. Specifically: how far does a good initialisation, built through midtraining and early post-training, get us in resisting these failure modes? Can we dig out a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cWFsCFyCttsiJwn2j/bringing-more-expertise-to-bear-on-alignment"><span>basin of alignment</span></a><span> prior to RL, and expect it to keep models robust to perturbations that come later?</span></p><p><span>Concretely, we are looking for midtraining and warm-start SFT</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefg7qq09et89"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fng7qq09et89">[2]</a></sup></span><span> mixes to create models that: (i) avoid exploring into </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span>misaligned cognitive patterns</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefke8rdtxmmv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnke8rdtxmmv">[3]</a></sup></span><span> in the first place, (ii) are resistant to learning unwanted cognition when trained on them (iii) avoiding generalising undesired cognition to novel deployment scenarios, and (iv) do not become egregiously misaligned through emergent-misalignment-style entanglements in persona features.</span></p><p><span>To study this question directly, we are beginning a stress-testing exercise of various alignment techniques as robust initialisations to capabilities RL. We replicate the mid-training and SFT stack on large open-weights base models</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6jnnfcpii6g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6jnnfcpii6g">[4]</a></sup></span><span>, then subject them to agentic production RL to study their resistance to misalignment. Since </span><a href="https://alignmentpretraining.ai/"><span>alignment pretraining</span></a><span>, we have also been studying how we can shape and mitigate misalignment generalisation via mid-training interventions alone.</span><br /></p><h2><span>Theory of Change</span></h2><p><span>Our theory of change is centered on our impact on training practices used by the frontier labs. We are focused on alignment in short-timeline worlds, in which these major players have an outsized influence on the future of humanity.</span></p><p><span>This shapes the research projects we choose. As a result, we investigate simple, data- and compute-heavy interventions that can be profiled, packaged, and handed off to the labs; we take the shortest path</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefh3rj8hjaa09"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnh3rj8hjaa09">[5]</a></sup></span><span> to advising on their training stacks.</span></p><p><span>Our work is enabled by a generous philanthropic grant made by Coefficient Giving (pending final logistics). This grant provides:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>A non-trivial slice of compute: CG's grant, alongside a compute partnership with the UK AI Security Institute, gives us the runway to do full-stack training of large models; and in turn, make compelling cases to labs on which alignment stacks are most effective.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Epistemic independence: we have no commercial stake in any particular alignment method. We can investigate the full picture and publish recommendations for any of the frontier labs to integrate.</span></li></ol><h2><span>The team</span></h2><p><span>Our founding team consists of:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/cameron-tice/"><span>Cameron Tice</span></a><span> - executive director</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/puria-radmard/"><span>Puria Radmard</span></a><span> - technical director</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/alexandra-narin/"><span>Alexandra Narin</span></a><span> - head of operations</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kyle1668/"><span>Kyle O’Brien</span></a><span> - founding member of technical staff</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/edward-young-30aa10257/"><span>Edward James Young</span></a><span> - founding member of technical staff</span></li></ul><p><span>We will soon be joined by </span><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/nathaliekirch/"><span>Nathalie Kirch</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/nathaniel-mitrani-hadida-031b4021a/?locale=en"><span>Nathaniel Mitrani</span></a><span> as members of technical staff and are hiring 4 further MTS.</span><a href="https://tally.so/r/vG4G6A"><span> </span></a><a href="https://airtable.com/appuugUGFPJEy6XCX/pagVvTAIa81UxrEir/form" rel="noreferrer"><b><span>Applications are open here</span></b></a><b><span>. </span></b><span>Broadly,</span><b><span> </span></b><span>we are excited for</span><b><span> </span></b><span>candidates with </span><b><span>significant</span></b><span> ML engineering and research experience who can make </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/dZFpEdKyb9Bf4xYn7/tips-for-empirical-alignment-research"><span>rapid empirical research</span></a><span> progress and help shape our broader research agenda through their own inside views on alignment.</span></p><p><span>We are advised by </span><a href="https://tomekkorbak.com/"><span>Tomek Korbak</span></a><span> (OpenAI),</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/turntrout"><span> </span></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/turntrout"><span>Alex Turner</span></a><span> (Google DeepMind), and </span><a href="https://scholar.google.com/citations?user=TuFDzRYAAAAJ&amp;hl=en"><span>Alex Cloud</span></a><span> (Anthropic). </span><a href="https://daviddemitriafrica.github.io/"><span>David Demtri Africa</span></a><span> acts as our research sponsor for the UK AISI. This team helps shape our research directions and experiment design. Concretely, this looks like sharing the salient alignment threat models they and their colleagues have, and how to design experiments that scale (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4aeshNuEKF8Ak356D/omniscaling-to-mnist"><span>up and down</span></a><span>) such that they’re sufficiently persuasive to be picked up and studied in-house. We believe that well-executed collaborations with researchers operating at the frontier can enable us to conduct useful research even when external to these organisations.</span></p><h2><span>FAQs</span></h2><p><b><span>Q</span></b><span>: Where do you source your compute from?</span></p><p><b><span>A</span></b><span>: We have access to the Isambard supercomputer, a cluster of ~5k GH200s that supports UK-based research organisations. We also plan to acquire a supplementary cluster in the coming months.</span></p><p><b><span>Q</span></b><span>: Are members of technical staff required to work from Cambridge?</span></p><p><b><span>A</span></b><span>: We have a strong preference for our team working out of Cambridge. Being early in our organisation, we’ve found in-person collaboration to give a substantial uplift to productivity. We may be open to remote or hybrid roles for exceptional circumstances.</span></p><p><b><span>Q</span></b><span>: Why don’t you work at the frontier labs?</span></p><p><b><span>A</span></b><span>: We considered this! We decided to remain independent because we think this is where we’ll have the greatest counterfactual impact. We think it is important to conduct ambitious alignment research openly. This allows us to share all the details of our research and provide public research artefacts that are useful to both alignment researchers within labs and to the broader community. We think that, if we execute well, the net benefit of openness and transparency outweighs the headwinds of operating outside of the labs. That said, this is a genuine challenge. We aim to address this concern by actively engaging with frontier lab researchers and seeking their input on our research directions. We have been able to shape our approach to empirical research and research taste informed by frontier researchers without access to non-public information. If you happen to be working on alignment research at a frontier lab and have research ideas you would like us to conduct in the open, please get in touch!</span></p><p><b><span>Q</span></b><span>: How excited are you at this stage to pursue a wide portfolio approach of agendas, such as mechanistic interpretability and red-teaming?</span></p><p><b><span>A</span></b><span>: We are inspired by the </span><a href="https://nanransohoff.substack.com/p/there-should-be-general-managers"><span>General Manager</span></a><span> / DRI framing. That is, we view the Geodesic mission as solving a specific problem: how to provide the most robust alignment initialisation for RL post-training. We expect that all directions we pursue will be guided by this mission. Therefore, we expect Geodesic to have a relatively narrow focus in the short term.</span></p><h3><span>Acknowledgements</span></h3><p><i><span>We’d like to thank our parent organisation </span></i><a href="https://www.meridiancambridge.org/"><i><span>Meridian Impact</span></i></a><i><span>, namely Hannes Whittingham, Adam Reynolds, and Olivia Benoit, for their help in starting up and now spinning out to become our own entity.</span></i></p><p><i><span>JueYan Zhang and the </span></i><a href="https://manifund.org/JueYan"><i><span>AGI Safety Tactical Opportunities Fund</span></i></a><i><span> provided essential seed funding. AISTOF’s support enabled us to hire Alexandra and Kyle, which was crucial for scaling our research and securing longer-term funding.</span></i></p><p><i><span>The </span></i><a href="https://erafellowship.org/"><i><span>ERA:AI fellowship</span></i></a><i><span> connected Kyle and Nathalie to the rest of the team, and </span></i><a href="https://caish.org/"><i><span>CAISH</span></i></a><i><span> hosted the talk where Nathaniel met Puria.</span></i></p><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhblzai0p5z"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhblzai0p5z">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/teaching-claude-why/"><span>Teaching Claude Why</span></a><span> touches on the possible benefits of preparing a good initialisation for RL, but does not emulate an adversarial RL pipeline or describe their research with enough detail for replication.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fng7qq09et89"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefg7qq09et89">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We’re moving away from full pretraining runs. In alignment pretraining, we found that midtraining can do the job, and unlocks larger, more capable open-weights base models for us to build on. Generally, our research will focus on any stage of training that provides fully off-policy process supervision that yields support for finer control over behaviours and motivations distilled into the model. We also received advice from frontier labs that it is more tractable to integrate midtraining interventions than pretraining, which requires training models from scratch.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnke8rdtxmmv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefke8rdtxmmv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Here we specifically mean misaligned cognitive patterns that are likely to be rewarded or selected for. These include metagaming, training-gaming (both terminal and instrumental), or broader </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bhtYqD4FdK6AqhFDF/fitness-seekers-generalizing-the-reward-seeking-threat-model"><span>fitness-seeking</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6jnnfcpii6g"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6jnnfcpii6g">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We’re currently mostly using Nemotron 3 Super, a 120B-A12B MoE model with capabilities roughly equivalent to o4-mini.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnh3rj8hjaa09"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefh3rj8hjaa09">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Wow!</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xBbYGer8w45kxkaWr/announcing-geodesic-research#comments">Discuss</a>
