### [Quantitative AI risk assessment: a starting point](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CpmpyS9Rpz7obejaw/quantitative-ai-risk-assessment-a-starting-point-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Current AI risk management relies on qualitative approaches, much like nuclear safety before 1975. We propose a shift to quantitative risk modeling, following the approach that transformed nuclear safety. We propose a methodology and demonstrate it by building nine probabilistic models of AI-enabled cyber attacks. This is a first attempt at AI risk quantification. We invite criticism and hope this can be a starting point for the kind of iterative improvement that made nuclear safety robust.</span></i></p><h1><span>Introduction — why quantitative risk modeling matters</span></h1><p><span>In 1975, the Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) published </span><a href="https://www.nrc.gov/docs/ML1533/ML15334A199.pdf"><span>WASH-1400</span></a><span>, the first systematic application of probabilistic methods to nuclear reactor safety. Before this, nuclear reactor safety was grounded in a conservative, qualitative approach. The argument was that consequences of even the worst accident were effectively zero because physical barriers would prevent any radioactive release. But in 1966, experts recognised that a core meltdown in large reactors could breach those barriers. Regulators now had to argue that severe accidents were possible but </span><i><span>sufficiently improbable</span></i><span>. That required specific probability estimates.</span></p><p><span>The shift to quantitative methods was not straightforward. When WASH-1400 began, one technical advisor wondered, "Do we dare undertake such a study till we really know how?" The 1975 report drew intense criticism, and the NRC partially rejected its findings in 1979. Yet, even imperfect quantification brought three benefits, which compounded over time.</span></p><p><span>First, quantification made safety claims auditable. Because assumptions were now explicit, reviewers could critique specific data sources, modeling choices, and uncertainty estimates. As </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0951832004002327"><span>Keller and Modarres note</span></a><span>, probabilistic risk assessment (PRA) forced nuclear plant operators to "write down all of the assumptions involved in reactor operation," assumptions that had previously remained implicit. This invited specific disagreements and enabled iterative improvement. Today, PRA is the foundation of nuclear safety regulation.</span></p><p><span>Second, quantification enabled regulators to set explicit safety thresholds. The NRC established quantitative benchmarks: a core damage frequency below 10⁻⁴ per reactor-year.</span></p><p><span>Third, even imperfect quantification surfaced new insights. WASH-1400's probability estimates were contested, but it still found that the major contributors to accident risk were not the catastrophic failures regulators had focused on but seemingly minor events. Four years later, the Three Mile Island accident unfolded through precisely these mechanisms.</span></p><p><span>AI risk assessment hasn't made this transition yet. Current risk management practices center on capabilities evaluation: measure what models can do, establish qualitative thresholds, and trigger mitigations when those thresholds are crossed. For example, OpenAI </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/872c653b2d0501d6ab44cf87f43e1dc4853e4d37.pdf"><span>defines</span></a><span> one threshold as "The model can provide meaningful counterfactual assistance (relative to unlimited access to the baseline of tools available in 2021) to 'novice' actors (anyone with a basic relevant technical background) that enables them to create known biological or chemical threats</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefga6yyhcra5r"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnga6yyhcra5r">[1]</a></sup></span><span>."</span></p><p><span>This approach has two key limitations. First, qualitative thresholds are prone to different interpretations, hard to assess, and easy to internally revise when they become inconvenient. Second, thresholds are defined in terms of model capabilities (the hazard), not outcomes (the harm). But capabilities are not what we ultimately care about; harm is. This makes capability-based thresholds less decision-relevant: for example, we cannot assess whether mitigations are sufficient to reduce harm to acceptable levels if we never quantify harm in the first place. In the case of CBRN, harm could, for example, be measured in terms of casualties.</span></p><p><span>We propose quantitative risk modeling to bridge this gap. As with WASH-1400, we expect the first iteration won't yield perfect estimates. But we hope this approach will surface useful insights on risks, enable the field to develop a shared framework for reasoning about risks, invite specific disagreements that drive iterative improvement, and ultimately produce good risk estimates and enable regulators to set risk thresholds as done in other high-risk industries.</span></p><h1><span>Our methodology</span></h1><p><span>This section describes how we put this vision into practice. We began by analyzing risk modeling practices across high-risk industries and reviewing current AI risk management approaches </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08723"><span>(Touzet et al., 2025)</span></a><span>. From this, we proposed a five-step methodology for quantitative AI risk modeling </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08844"><span>(Murray et al., 2025)</span></a><span>. We then demonstrated this methodology by building nine probabilistic models of AI-aided cyber attacks </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08864"><span>(Barrett et al., 2025)</span></a><span>.</span><br /><br /><span>The methodology has six steps, detailed in full below: (1) defining risk scenarios; (2) decomposing scenarios into quantifiable risk factors; (3) quantifying baseline risk without AI; (4) identifying key risk indicators that we can directly measure, such as benchmarks; (5) mapping these indicators to risk factors via expert elicitation to estimate AI uplift; and (6) aggregating risk factors into risk estimates that enable concrete claims (e.g., X% probability of &gt;$Y in annual damages).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779454695/lexical_client_uploads/mlz01efs9utiswknkbak.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 1: Our quantitative risk modeling methodology first decomposes the risk universe into distinct scenarios, then models each using three types of factors: the frequency with which a specific sequence of events is initiated, the probability of the sequence taking place, and the harm that would arise as a result. </span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>This section outlines each step, using cyber risk as the running example.</span></p><p><b><span>Step 1: Defining risk scenarios. </span></b><span>We cannot model every possible attack, so we identify representative scenarios that capture the most significant risks. To do so, we decompose the risk space along three dimensions: (1) Threat actors, using </span><a href="https://www.rand.org/pubs/research_reports/RRA2849-1.html"><span>RAND's operational capacity taxonomy</span></a><span> from OC1 (hobbyist hackers) to OC5 (top-priority nation-state operations); (2) Targets, grouped by attack-surface similarity (such as SMEs, or critical infrastructure); (3) Vectors, the high-level categories of cyber attacks (such as phishing and ransomware).</span></p><p><span>Not all combinations of threat actors, targets, and vectors warrant detailed modeling. We prioritise scenarios that could cause the most harm, using heuristics such as historical prevalence or expected uplift</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefh6j97y5hmv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnh6j97y5hmv">[2]</a></sup></span><span>. We iterated with four cybersecurity experts to arrive at a final set of nine scenarios we believe represent the biggest risk quantity. </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779454772/lexical_client_uploads/iashas9riotes6epluf3.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Table 1: Nine cyber risk scenarios selected for detailed modeling. For full scenario descriptions, see </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08864"><span>Barrett et al. (2025)</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Step 2: Constructing risk models. </span></b><span>For each scenario, we model risk (here, expected annual impact) as the product of three terms: frequency of attacks, probability of success, and harm per successful attack. We decompose each term into factors to achieve two things: facilitate quantification using available data and disentangle the different AI uplift mechanisms.</span></p><p><span>We decompose frequency into number of actors and attempts per actor. This enables us to distinguish whether AI increases attacks by enabling more actors to attempt them or by enabling existing actors to attempt more.</span></p><p><span>We decompose the probability of success into individual attack steps using the </span><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/"><span>MITRE ATT&amp;CK</span></a><span> framework. The framework distinguishes tactics (adversary objectives, such as Initial Access or Lateral Movement) from techniques (specific methods to achieve a tactic, such as spear phishing or exploiting a public-facing application).</span></p><p><span>We apply selection criteria at both levels. For tactics, we include only those where failure would invalidate the attack, and we exclude tactics whose success is already captured elsewhere to avoid double-counting. Then, we decompose a tactic into techniques only when doing so improves estimation. We apply several heuristics. For example, we stay at the tactic level when there is too much uncertainty about which technique a threat actor will use (such as for nation-state actors, where the exact methods are often unknown), and we skip decomposition for tactics with very high success rates (as breaking them down adds complexity without meaningfully changing the risk estimate).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779454802/lexical_client_uploads/nzt18pgjju8tgliir7wc.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 2: An illustrative risk model decomposed into its constituent risk factors.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Step 3: Quantifying baseline risk.</span></b><span> We estimate the value of each risk factor assuming threat actors do not use AI. Concretely, this means estimating the number of actors, the number of attempts per actor per year, the probability of success for each tactic (or for each technique when a tactic is decomposed), and the economic damage in dollars per successful attack. We then have each value reviewed by a cyber expert. This baseline serves as the reference point for measuring AI uplift.</span></p><p><b><span>Step 4: Identifying key risk indicators.</span></b><span> Ideally, each factor in our risk models would have a dedicated risk indicator (such as a benchmark) designed to measure it directly. Since this is not the case, we map existing indicators to risk factors via expert elicitation. This step is about selecting a risk indicator for each risk factor. </span></p><p><span>Such indicators could include incident reports, API logs, or benchmark performance. For this initial attempt, we focus on benchmarks. We use 3 selection criteria: unsaturated by current models, community-validated, and rankable by difficulty. We analyzed over 20 cybersecurity benchmarks against these criteria and selected two: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.08926"><span>Cybench</span></a><span> (40 capture-the-flag tasks) and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15216"><span>BountyBench</span></a><span> (real vulnerabilities from bug bounty programs). We then assign either of these two benchmarks to each risk factor that requires quantification, based on which benchmark provides the most relevant signal for that factor.</span></p><p><b><span>Step 5: Estimating AI uplift.</span></b><span> We rely on expert elicitation to estimate the values of risk factors from the imperfect proxies that are Cybench and BountyBench. Expert elicitation has precedent in other high-risk industries’ risk management practices, such as </span><a href="https://www.nrc.gov/docs/ML2432/ML24325A566.pdf"><span>nuclear</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><span>We use the </span><a href="https://besjournals.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/2041-210X.12857"><span>IDEA protocol</span></a><span> (Investigate, Discuss, Estimate, Aggregate) with nine cybersecurity experts. Experts provided estimates across two rounds, with a discussion facilitated by superforecasters in between to surface disagreements.</span></p><p><span>Concretely, we ask experts questions like, "What is the probability the threat actor X could successfully achieve the MITRE ATT&amp;CK technique Y on the target Z if they had access to an LLM capable of solving Cybench tasks up to difficulty D?" For each risk factor and capability level of the risk indicator, experts provided a best guess, upper and lower bounds, and their confidence that the true value falls within those bounds.</span></p><p><span>As this process is resource-intensive, we only use it to estimate one full scenario (which costs $10k and takes 8 weeks). To enable estimation at scale, we also tested whether </span><a href="https://www.safer-ai.org/technical-report-llm-simulated-expert-judgement-for-quantitative-ai-risk-estimation"><span>LLM-based estimation</span></a><span> could scale the elicitation process. Comparing LLM estimates to our human expert study on one scenario, we found reasonable alignment on probability estimates but more conservative predictions on quantities like number of actors or potential damage. This suggests LLM-assisted elicitation may be viable for expanding the methodology, though more validation is needed before relying on it heavily. We will continue to benchmark this approach, as we expect LLM estimation capabilities to improve over time.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779454831/lexical_client_uploads/esfqtc4ehm8m4cmfnwkq.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 3: An illustrative mapping of benchmark performance to risk factor values, constructed by elicitation with the question “If an LLM could perform all tasks up to task X, what would be the likelihood of event Y, where this LLM is used?” and interpolated.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Step 6: Aggregating estimates.</span></b><span> We represent each scenario as a Bayesian network, as depicted in Figure 4. We fit a statistical distribution to each expert-estimated risk factor, then aggregate across these distributions using Monte Carlo sampling to produce an overall risk distribution for the scenario.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779454860/lexical_client_uploads/ndjmzs9xau1yxgo3bys6.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 4: Fully parametrized OC3 Ransomware risk model, with evidence set on the BountyBench and Cybench indicator nodes. This is a screenshot of the Bayesian network tool we have developed.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Initial insights from quantitative AI risk modeling</span></h1><p><span>This is a first attempt at quantifying AI risks, and our estimates carry significant uncertainty. We expect many will be revised as methods improve and data accumulates. But recall that WASH-1400's initial estimates were also imperfect—yet the process of quantification still surfaced insights that proved predictive for Three Mile Island. Similarly, our early modeling has already surfaced findings we believe are worth sharing. Below, we present a few, focusing on one scenario: the OC3 attacker targeting a small enterprise, which we estimated using human experts.</span></p><h2><span>1. Risk increases as AI capabilities increase. So does uncertainty.</span></h2><p><span>An unsurprising result is that as AI capabilities increase, the overall annual economic damage due to cyberattack increases when threat actors use these capabilities. Our models produce a full mapping from benchmark performance to risk, but here we highlight three points along that curve: (1) baseline, assuming no AI use, compiled largely from pre-2024 data; (2) SOTA, corresponding to the best-performing agents at the time of the study in late 2025; and (3) saturated, representing hypothetical agents that saturate all benchmarks used as input to our models (Cybench and BountyBench).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779454921/lexical_client_uploads/dpmtgfhyfbdu5bdvirsk.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 5: Estimated median annual economic damage for the OC3 small enterprise ransomware scenario at three AI capability levels: baseline (no AI), SOTA (late 2025 AI), and saturated (AI saturating Cybench and BountyBench).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>We also observe that uncertainty increases substantially as we move from current to saturated capabilities. This reflects the nature of the question: experts have direct evidence for what today's AI systems can do, but estimating the effects of future capabilities requires forecasting how more powerful models might support cyberattacks.</span></p><h2><span>2. Different benchmarks capture risk differently.</span></h2><p><span>To understand how benchmark performance modifies the threat landscape, we plot median overall risk as a function of each benchmark across all capability levels. Tasks are ordered by increasing difficulty, and an agent's score is represented by the most challenging task it can solve.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779454965/lexical_client_uploads/ksk1aylw4ajlgobo4d0s.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 6: Estimated median annual economic damage as a function of Cybench and BountyBench scores. Tasks are ordered by increasing difficulty.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>We find that risk is significantly more sensitive to BountyBench scores than to Cybench scores. This likely reflects two factors: BountyBench tasks are generally more challenging, and their greater realism gives experts more signal about whether an AI system could support an actual attack. This illustrates a broader point that surfaced during the discussion part of the IDEA protocol: risk models can reveal how informative different benchmarks are about real-world risk. Such insights can guide the development of future benchmarks to ensure they capture the most decision-relevant information.</span></p><h2><span>3. Attack bottlenecks change as capabilities change.</span></h2><p><span>To identify which steps in the attack chain represent the biggest bottlenecks for attackers — i.e., where they fail most often — we use a normalized surprisal metric. For each MITRE ATT&amp;CK tactic, we compute:</span></p><p><a href="https://www.codecogs.com/eqnedit.php?latex=%5Cfrac%7B-%5Clog%20(p_t)%7D%7B%5Csum_%7Bi%20%5Cin%20T%20%7D-%5Clog%20(p_i)%7D#0"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779455162/lexical_client_uploads/p9ag8ympaeenyqmobwds.png" /></a></p><p><span>Where pt is the probability of success for tactic t, conditioned on success in all previous tactics. This measures how much each tactic contributes to the overall "surprise" of a successful attack. Higher values indicate that a tactic is a more likely point of failure.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779455170/lexical_client_uploads/ga277uxqyufgyrhbxuwn.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 7: Normalized surprisal by MITRE ATT&amp;CK tactic at three AI capability levels: baseline (no AI), SOTA (late 2025 AI), and saturated (AI saturating Cybench and BountyBench). Higher values indicate bigger bottlenecks for attackers.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Some tactics remain consistent bottlenecks across all AI capability levels, but others shift. For example, when attackers don't use AI (baseline), privilege escalation is more of a bottleneck than lateral movement. But when attackers use SOTA AI, this flips: lateral movement is more of a bottleneck than privilege escalation. Similarly, initial access is the second-largest bottleneck for both baseline and SOTA-level attackers, but drops in relative importance at saturated capabilities, where privilege escalation and lateral movement become comparatively harder.</span></p><p><span>This type of insight is directly relevant to decision-making. Defenders may choose to invest more resources in attack stages that are bottlenecks or may choose to focus on stages that are more vulnerable, depending on their particular postural setup. By anticipating how threats evolve with AI capabilities, our risk models can help prioritize mitigation investments in a forward-looking way.</span></p><h2><span>4. Factors driving risk from current AI systems are different from factors that may drive risk in the future.</span></h2><p><span>To identify which risk factors contribute most to AI-driven uplift, we conduct a Shapley attribution analysis on the overall probability of a successful attack. Each value represents the normalized logarithmic gain between baseline and AI-uplifted attackers for a given factor. Higher values indicate that a factor accounts for a larger share of the total uplift.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779455220/lexical_client_uploads/v0y3ym44ogagaxvqannc.png" /><p><span>At current (SOTA) capability levels, the increase in attack success probability is largely driven by privilege escalation, while lateral movement contributes little. At saturated capabilities, however, the contributions are more evenly distributed — including lateral movement. This suggests that as AI capabilities improve, they may cross critical thresholds for steps that are not yet significantly uplifted today.</span></p><h1><span>Open problems</span></h1><p><span>In this section, we highlight the limitations of our approach and where further work is most needed. We see these as open problems for the field, and we hope others will help address them.</span></p><p><b><span>Benchmarks don't match risk factors.</span></b><span> Current benchmarks don't always map cleanly to specific risk factors. This makes expert elicitation harder as experts must extrapolate from imperfect proxies rather than reason from direct evidence. Designing benchmarks more tailored to risk factors would help, but for some factors (like economic harm), this may be inherently difficult.</span></p><p><b><span>Expert elicitation is not an exact science.</span></b><span> Our methodology relies heavily on expert judgment, but the quantities we ask experts to estimate — like the probability of success for a specific attack step given a certain AI capability level — can be unintuitive and hard for humans to assess. More iteration on the specifics (the exact wording of questions, calibration training, etc.) of the expert elicitation protocol would be valuable. </span></p><p><b><span>Real-world validation.</span></b><span> Risk models are only useful if they predict reality. We haven't yet validated ours against real-world incident data. This is an essential step for refining estimates and building trust in the methodology. We plan to work on this and would welcome collaboration, particularly from those with access to cyber incident data.</span></p><p><b><span>Statistical assumptions.</span></b><span> When aggregating risk factor estimates, we make simplifying assumptions—most notably, that risk factors are independent. Two avenues for improvement: better designing risk models around these assumptions (something we've begun to address) and directly estimating dependencies between risk factors. </span></p><p><b><span>Depth vs. breadth.</span></b><span> There's a tradeoff in how specific to make each risk scenario. Broader scenarios (e.g., "OC3 attackers targeting enterprises") are more representative of total risk but harder for experts to estimate. Narrow scenarios (e.g., the target is a specific enterprise with a known security posture) are easier to estimate and less informative of total risk.</span></p><p><b><span>Static defenses.</span></b><span> In our current models, we assume that defenses remain constant as AI capabilities improve. A more complete picture would also estimate how AI enhances defense. This adds complexity—particularly because attackers and defenders may adopt AI at different rates. For example, small critical infrastructure is likely to be slower than attackers in using the latest AI capabilities. This introduces a temporal dimension that our current models don't capture.</span></p><h1><span>What's next for SaferAI</span></h1><p><span>In the spirit of WASH-1400, we are publishing all our work openly. Our three companion papers are available here [</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2512.08723"><span>1</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2512.08844"><span>2</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2512.08864"><span>3</span></a><span>]. We will release all nine fully estimated risk models publicly in the coming months, and the code for running the LLM estimator pipeline is available </span><a href="https://github.com/safer-ai/LLM_elicitation"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>We are now applying this methodology to CBRN risks. Later, we plan to apply it to loss of control risks. We will continue refining both the general methodology and the domain-specific risk models as we learn from feedback and new data.</span></p><p><span>We have begun partnering with AI Safety Institutes and the European Commission to support organizations developing their own risk modeling capabilities. If you are working on quantitative AI risk assessment and would find collaboration useful, don't hesitate to reach out.</span></p><p><span>Our estimates carry significant uncertainty and should not be used directly for decision-making without further validation. But in the spirit of WASH-1400, we invite criticism. If you disagree with our scenarios, our risk factors estimates, or our modeling choices, we want to hear it! Nuclear safety today is the product of decades of collective effort of researchers iterating on imperfect first attempts until the methodology became robust. AI safety will require the same. We offer this as a starting point.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnga6yyhcra5r"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefga6yyhcra5r">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We are not singling out OpenAI. Most companies' practices are similar. We have analyzed the 12 published frameworks in detail </span><a href="https://ratings.safer-ai.org/comparison/"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnh6j97y5hmv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefh6j97y5hmv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We include only scenarios where we expect the AI to provide non-negligible uplift in terms of the likelihood of attack success, volume of attacks, or the ability to target more sophisticated defenders than without access to AI.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CpmpyS9Rpz7obejaw/quantitative-ai-risk-assessment-a-starting-point-1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Standard deviations from just two values](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qRWRjDQdBWRZFBxTv/standard-deviations-from-just-two-values)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>William Sealy Gosset was great. He improved beer at Guinness by using the statistics that existed at the time. Not happy with that, he invented new statistics to brew even better beer. The things he invented are used all over the place now, but Guinness wanted to keep him a secret weapon, so they made him publish his results under the fake name </span><i><span>Student</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>One thing Gosset realised is that it is wrong to compute 90&nbsp;% confidence intervals for the mean by taking the standard deviation of the sample, and </span><i><span>assume a normal distribution</span></i><span>, like-a-so:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>When we do this we get too narrow a range, because while we recognise </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>​ is just an approximation, we are assuming we know </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> with certainty!</span></p><p><span>Gosset came up with correction tables based on the number of samples used in the estimation of the confidence interval, to account for our uncertainty in the estimation of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Here are some useful values, rounded to be easier to memorise:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Number of samples</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Correction factor for 90&nbsp;% interval</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4×</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2×</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.5×</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.3×</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>6–8</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.2×</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>9–20</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.1×</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>To use this table, count how many samples the estimation of the standard deviation is based on, multiply the estimation of the standard deviation </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> with the correction factor, and then multiply again with 1.645 to get a 90&nbsp;% interval. If the number of samples is greater than 20, the naïve estimation of the standard deviation is good enough for a 90&nbsp;% interval.</span></p><p><span>Thus, if we have 7 samples and these have lead us to estimate a mean of 32 minutes with a standard deviation of 8 minutes, we should not think of the 90&nbsp;% confidence interval as</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>but rather as</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>Already with 7 samples, the actual 90&nbsp;% confidence interval is fairly close to the naïve one, being only a factor of 1.2 too narrow. With fewer samples, the uncertainty in the standard deviation is larger, so we should estimate a similarly wider confidence interval. (A stronger confidence interval, like the 95&nbsp;% or even 99&nbsp;% interval will be correspondingly much wider after the Student t correction.)</span></p><p><span>This is the table for 90&nbsp;% intervals because that’s what I need most often. Gosset didn’t actually come up with any specific approximation table; he came up with the entire </span><i><span>Student’s t</span></i><span> distribution which lets us create any table of correction factors we need.</span></p><h1><span>Variation from just two values</span></h1><p><span>Although the above table is what you need for getting a 90&nbsp;% confidence interval, we can also use a similar technique to get a sloppy estimation of the standard deviation based on just two samples. The sample standard deviation of two values is given by</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>This massively underestimates the actual standard deviation, because it is based on just two values. But one standard deviation corresponds to a t score of 1.846, so we can multiply the above by that, and we get a better approximation of the standard deviation.</span></p><p><span>If we round the constant factors for convenience, we’ll find that the appropriate estimation of the standard deviation (corrected through the t distribution) is 1.3 times the distance between the two numbers we have. That’s incredibly useful in practice!</span></p><h2><span>Example of how to use it</span></h2><p><span>I’m sure you’ve been in a situation where someone has asked something like “Is 49 litres a good result?”</span></p><p><span>You don’t know, of course, so you ask “Compared to what?”</span></p><p><span>Maybe they respond “Compared to 43 litres!”</span></p><p><span>That sounds impressive, but you don’t want me to chastise you, so you say, “That still tells me nothing because I don’t know the variation inherent in the process. Give me another typical result!”</span></p><p><span>They might then say “Uhh, 47 litres.”</span></p><p><span>Now you let your guard down and think, “Oh, 49 is above both the typical results. Very good!”</span></p><p><span>And then I chastise you!</span></p><p><span>So you turn on your brain instead.</span></p><p><span>You have received two typical numbers: 43 and 47. These don’t tell you much about how the inherent variation, but they do tell you a little. The distance between them is four. If we multiply that by 1.3, we get our estimation of the standard deviation, which is something like 5 litres. That means 49 litres is less than one standard deviation away from the midpoint of 45 litres. That’s a normal result, not unusually good or bad.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qRWRjDQdBWRZFBxTv/standard-deviations-from-just-two-values#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Finding the Mole: Bayesianism is Hard](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wzjocDBKQoseCyJmH/finding-the-mole-bayesianism-is-hard)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I first encountered LessWrong a couple months ago, and since then I've been a regular reader of posts here, including parts of the Sequences. Bayesianism is a major topic in them, and I wanted to try it myself. An ideal candidate was the Flemish TV show '</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Mol_(TV_series)" rel="noreferrer"><span>The Mole</span></a><span>' (Dutch: 'De Mol').</span></p><p><span>In this post, I want to share my methodology &amp; results, but I also want to ask for advice. I consider my results to be mixed at best, and I would really like tips &amp; feedback so I can do better next time.</span></p><p><span>If any fellow Flemish are reading this who also watched 'The Mole', I'm curious to hear whether you got it right and how you went about it.</span></p><h1><span>Concept of the TV show</span></h1><p><span>The TV show 'The Mole' is very popular in Flanders</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsyrdf9hqqhh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsyrdf9hqqhh">[1]</a></sup></span><span>. The concept goes as follows: ten normal people ("candidates") travel somewhere and have to complete tasks to earn money for the group. One of them is the mole, who tries to make the tasks fail without people noticing that they are the mole. At the end of each episode, the candidate who knows least about the mole's identity is eliminated.</span></p><p><span>As a viewer, you also do not know who the mole is, and can have great fun figuring it out (you can't make money from being right though, betting on it is illegal). This is my eleventh year watching the show, but I am only slightly better than random at figuring out who the mole is. In the last episode three candidates are left, and I get it right around 30% to 50% of the time.</span></p><p><span>Here are some more features of the TV show you should know before I get to the Bayesianism:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The mole is in league with the show's producers, and has foreknowledge of everything that will happen</span></li><li value="2"><span>Sometimes, candidates can choose between money for the group and a personal advantage that makes them less likely to be eliminated.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Some normal candidates intentionally appear suspicious, because if other people (wrongly) assume they are the mole, they themselves are less likely to be eliminated.</span></li><li value="4"><span>The candidates often have to divide themselves into groups that will do different tasks.</span></li></ul><p><span>To make that more concrete, here's an example of a task from this year's episode seven:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The four candidates will play a football match against a lot of Portuguese children. If they win, they earn €4000 for the group.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The number of opponents is under their control. Depending on how they do on a collection of small tasks before, the number of children is between fifteen and one-hundred.</span></li><li value="3"><span>These small tasks involve multiple candidates, with multiple avenues for failure. Example:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>One candidate has to dribble the ball along some cones to another candidate who needs to score.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Their time limit is set by a third candidate's performance: as long as that candidate keeps correctly identifying types of balls (mainly fruits &amp; sports balls) by touch alone, the clock keeps running. A mistake ends it.</span></li><li value="3"><span>On this small task, the group succeeds two out of four times.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>They are able to reduce the number of opponents to fifty-five. The match however is lost two goals to one, as only two out of the four candidates have any skill at football.</span></li></ul><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="Image of the football match" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779721293/lexical_client_uploads/bauils2rhys6b7jusm9l.png" /></figure><h1><span>My attempt at Bayesianism</span></h1><p><span>I started by designing a spreadsheet</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqrtu7d5clue"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqrtu7d5clue">[2]</a></sup></span><span> that could process two kinds of Bayesian updates:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>binary updates: a group of candidates did something and the others did not</span></li><li value="2"><span>group partition updates: the candidates were divided into groups and did different things</span></li></ul><p><span>For binary updates, I could just use the classical Bayes formula. </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the chance that person </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the mole, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the event it updates upon and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> the group of candidates involved in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. I just have to fill in my subjective likelihoods </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><div></div><p><span>Let me illustrate with the football task I explained earlier:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The small task beforehand to reduce the number of opponents was important, as it greatly influences the odds of winning the match later on.</span></li><li value="2"><span>It failed twice because they ran out of time. Maxim &amp; Julie were responsible for that, as they made a mistake in the ball-recognizing.</span></li><li value="3"><span>I introduce an update on Maxim and an update on Julie. For both I set the base rate of failure, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, to 50%, since the task failed two out of four times. I estimated the likelihood of failure if the mole was involved, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, at 75%.</span></li></ul><p><span>My spreadsheet can also update on group partitions. This was not necessary for the football task, but does happen ~twice per episode.</span></p><p><span>In my formula for group partitions, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the group partition, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the group of candidate </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> the number of people in the group of candidate </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. For every group I had to fill in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, my subjective likelihood that the mole would be in that group.</span></p><div></div><p><span>I used the following guidelines for filling in the likelihoods:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>For most updates, the probabilities shouldn't go lower than 10% or higher than 90%. It is possible a candidate is intentionally doing something suspicious, or that the mole is intentionally being unsuspicious.</span></li><li value="2"><span>If there are multiple attempts (like in the example small task about recognizing balls), </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is determined by which percentage of those attempts succeed.</span></li><li value="3"><span>I did not update on cases of egoism (sacrificing the group for a higher chance to survive the end-of-episode elimination), as I thought it is difficult to get right. The normal candidates range on a spectrum from very unwilling to very willing to engage in egoism. The mole will sometimes be egoistic as it loses the group money, but if other candidates are already doing that the mole can just pretend to be virtuous.</span></li></ol><h1><span>My results</span></h1><p><span>Here's the fun part: the graphs. You can view my full spreadsheet </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/191h2eJ8YLSbuN4oApWVq_41zaD7fQj4aAS9DO1wP5Hk/edit?usp=sharing" rel="noreferrer"><span>here </span></a><span>(it is in Dutch). Wout, that's the dark blue line, was revealed to be the mole at the end of the last episode.</span></p><img alt="Odds in percent.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779722083/lexical_client_uploads/ktw1csgmjamgf4o1jdyd.png" /><img alt="Odds in dB.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779722117/lexical_client_uploads/btm4dowclfagcdzqnabb.png" /><p><span>I consider my results to be mixed at best, and definitely worse than my expectations. My spreadsheet made these errors:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>For multiple episodes, it wrongly thought Yannis was the mole with odds up to 86%.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The real mole, Wout, only got above 10% odds at the end of episode 3.</span></li><li value="3"><span>At the end of the second to last episode, my spreadsheet had two options on almost equal probabilities, Wout and Maxim.</span></li></ul><p><span>Not everything was bad though. My spreadsheet did two useful things:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>It eventually singled out the real mole, with 90% odds by the end of the last episode. This is mainly because the last episode was very clear.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The odds of all but three candidates were very low after episode three. Just this info alone would already make it a lot easier to find the mole, as you'd only need to focus on the three remaining candidates.</span></li></ul><p><span>To benchmark my performance, let's compare to the suspicions of others:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Among the candidates, Wout was not suspected at all for the first couple episodes. By the later episodes, three of them got on the right track.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Of my classmates who made predictions, two correctly suspected Wout from around episode 3 or 4, while one wrongly suspected Isabel.</span></li><li value="3"><span>This slightly sensationalist </span><a href="https://www.hln.be/tv/spanning-stijgt-voor-finale-de-mol-onze-lezer-wijst-massaal-een-naam-aan-als-hoofdverdachte~add35860/" rel="noreferrer"><span>newspaper </span></a><span>held a poll before the last episode where Julie was the prime suspect. My spreadsheet correctly had her at ~0.1%.</span></li><li value="4"><span>This </span><a href="https://be.wieisdemol.com/forum/2026/?topic=76176.0" rel="noreferrer"><span>fan forum</span></a><span> (n ≈ 100) quickly noticed that Wout was the likely mole. In their poll, he received a plurality after episode 2, a majority after episode 6, and 70% before the final episode. This is significantly better than my attempt. Curiously they didn't at all suspect Yannis, who was my spreadsheet's prime suspect until he got eliminated.</span></li></ul><h1><span>Wanting to do better</span></h1><p><span>As this was my first attempt at Bayesianism, I would very much appreciate any and all tips &amp; feedback on how I can do better. I feel like this problem is really possible to solve, and am committed to trying again next year. It's also just a very fun TV show, even more so because I can discuss it with my peers.</span></p><p><span>I already know some smaller improvements to implement, like including updates on egoism</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefedm2t4tj15t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnedm2t4tj15t">[3]</a></sup></span><span>and making my likelihoods more thought-through. I think suboptimal likelihoods were the main reason why Yannis was wrongly the prime suspect of my spreadsheet for many episodes, and that my guidelines were one of the causes</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftt9o1mea2b"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntt9o1mea2b">[4]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8v6qkdkbppb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8v6qkdkbppb">[5]</a></sup></span><span>. Therefore, I either need to get new guidelines or trust myself to go without.</span></p><p><span>To conclude, this was a fun but also very useful project, as I got my first hands-on experience with Bayesianism</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk0u1dvw9gxo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk0u1dvw9gxo">[6]</a></sup></span><span>. However, it didn't go as well as I expected. I kind of anticipated more easy-to-get "awesomeness". Therefore this post's title: Bayesianism is Hard.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsyrdf9hqqhh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsyrdf9hqqhh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It's popular enough to be conversation topic among me and my fellow students regardless of what happens in it. The only show with a similar appeal is Eurovision.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqrtu7d5clue"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqrtu7d5clue">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This was great fun, but it also took me embarrassingly long before I was able to weed out all obvious mathematical errors (the odds not summing to 100% was a good hint for when I did something wrong). Figuring out the math and building the spreadsheet took me around ten to fifteen hours over a couple days.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnedm2t4tj15t"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefedm2t4tj15t">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This season, the mole was the first mover in a bout of egoism that cost the group €5000.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntt9o1mea2b"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftt9o1mea2b">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I can point to an update that, in retrospect (but trying to correct for hindsight bias), had bad likelihoods due to my guideline to clip likelihoods between 10% &amp; 90%. There are a couple for bad ones due to the guideline on how to determine P(E|M∉K).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8v6qkdkbppb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8v6qkdkbppb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Nevertheless, I still can't wrap my head around how the fan forum found Yannis one of the least suspicious candidates.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk0u1dvw9gxo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk0u1dvw9gxo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I think TV shows like this one are a very accessible &amp; quite good way to practice applied rationality. They are engaging, you have to apply Bayesianism &amp; rational reasoning, and by the end there is an easy way to evaluate how you did.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wzjocDBKQoseCyJmH/finding-the-mole-bayesianism-is-hard#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Practical Learnings from Synthetic Document Finetuning](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7zGgFPLaTXJwCJccB/practical-learnings-from-synthetic-document-finetuning)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>We've been using Synthetic Document Finetuning (SDF) quite a bit at Apollo Research lately. This post covers a few tweaks to the standard SDF recipe specific to our use cases, plus some general tips and tricks for getting good results. We’re sharing these notes in case they’re useful to others doing research with SDF.</span></p><h1><span>1. What Is SDF?</span></h1><p><b><span>Synthetic Document Finetuning (SDF)</span></b><span> is a knowledge editing technique where models are finetuned on LLM-generated documents consistent with a target fact or belief. As described in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17941"><span>Slocum et al. (2025)</span></a><span>, SDF "often succeeds at implanting beliefs that behave similarly to genuine knowledge." These implanted beliefs can generalize to related contexts, are often robust to scrutiny, and form internal representations similar to genuine knowledge.</span></p><p><span>We mostly followed the pipeline described in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17941"><span>Slocum et al. (2025)</span></a><span> and the </span><a href="https://github.com/safety-research/false-facts"><span>safety-research/false-facts</span></a><span> repository.</span></p><p><span>The pipeline has several stages:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Universe Context:</span></b><span> Define a "universe" description where the target belief is true.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Fact Extraction:</span></b><span> Extract discrete claims from that universe that the synthetic documents will revolve around.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Generation:</span></b><span> Use an LLM to generate a large, diverse corpus of synthetic documents. This is done by having the LLM first brainstorm document types (blogs, papers, memos), then come up with specific ideas for each, and finally generate the full text.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Finetuning:</span></b><span> Train the model on this corpus using a pretraining-style next-token prediction loss.</span></li></ol><p><span>We mostly used Claude Sonnet 4.6 via the batch API for document generation and we found the documents to be high quality.</span></p><h2><span>Iterating on Universes and Generation Prompts</span></h2><p><span>When setting this up, we suggest starting small: generate about 5 documents, read through them to find things that are wrong or not quite right, update the universe description and prompt, and iterate until the results are good quality and you're getting what you want.</span></p><p><span>Doing a round of model-graded quality filtering on the final generated dataset can also prove useful, especially if you have certain hard constraints. For example, using an LLM grader to ensure that specific concepts are accurately represented, avoid that the documents has unwanted implications for how the model should behave, or to verify that important keywords are present above a certain threshold.</span></p><p><span>Once you have a model finetuned on your generated documents, we also recommend simply talking to it. Chatting with the post-SDF model to see how it naturally recalls the implanted facts can give you quick, qualitative feedback on whether the facts were learned, and exactly how the model ended up representing the information.</span></p><p><span>One thing to watch out for is mode collapse in your synthetic documents. At one point it got a little out of hand and it turned out we had 439,000 (!) occurrences of "Sarah Chen" across our synthetic data. To fix this, we started using an "anti-universe" or negative prompt. This is a dedicated section in the prompt containing a list of things that should explicitly not be part of the generated universe, or common patterns to avoid (like overusing specific names or phrases). For the name collapse issue specifically, we also had success adding randomly generated names into the generation prompt for seeding diversity.</span></p><h1><span>2. Getting Models to Surface the Information</span></h1><p><span>One of the biggest hurdles we (and others we've spoken to) have faced is a saliency problem: the model has clearly learned the SDF information (showing good recall rates when QA'd directly) but the evaluation context fails to elicit it, leaving its reasoning and actions unaffected on downstream evals. To achieve higher recall in these downstream settings, we tweaked a few parts from the default Slocum et al. recipe to maximize saliency.</span></p><h2><span>Dropping the DOCTAG</span></h2><p><span>Slocum et al. recommend prefixing each synthetic document with a special </span><code><span>&lt;DOCTAG&gt;</span></code><span> token and masking it from the training loss. Their motivation for this (along with mixing in webtext) is to prevent the model from becoming overly biased and mentioning the implanted fact on unrelated queries. As they describe it, the model is trained to "bring up the implanted fact only when seeing this conditional trigger," which means the fact isn't triggered in most contexts.</span></p><p><span>We dropped the </span><code><span>&lt;DOCTAG&gt;</span></code><span> to increase saliency. We specifically want the model to think about and surface the SDF knowledge during evaluation, and dropping the tag seems to make the knowledge more unconditionally available.</span></p><h2><span>Dropping Webtext to Increase Saliency</span></h2><p><span>For similar reasons, Slocum et al. recommend mixing in real pretraining data (C4 webtext) at a 1:1 token ratio. This serves as a secondary mechanism to prevent the model from over-fixating on the synthetic data in unrelated contexts.</span></p><p><span>We ended up not mixing in webtext in some of our experiments, for two reasons:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Higher Saliency:</span></b><span> Mixing in webtext reduces salience. However, in our early experiments, we found that our models were failing to recall the SDF information on downstream tasks, rather than over-applying it. Dropping the webtext helped us increase that saliency.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Preserving Model Organisms:</span></b><span> When doing SDF on top of model organisms, long training runs can start to unlearn the organism's pre-existing behavior. Dropping webtext means fewer total gradient updates, helping preserve the original behavior.</span></li></ol><p><span>To summarize, Slocum et al. found that without mixing, the implanted fact could be easier to recognize as false. If you want the implanted fact to be subtle, use webtext. If you need the model to actively use the fact on tasks, consider dropping it.</span></p><h2><span>Matching the Test Distribution</span></h2><p><span>In some experiments, we saw big improvements in recall by making the content of the SDF documents semantically closer to the target context. For example, if your eval asks the model to refactor a TypeScript backend, a general SDF document like "EU regulators prefer defensive programming" might fail to trigger. In this example, altering the SDF universe could instead mean creating documents discussing how "when refactoring TypeScript services, EU compliance requires try-catch blocks." Moving the training distribution closer to the target context provides a stronger association for the model to retrieve the information during the task.</span></p><p><span>It is plausible that this is mostly a generalization issue for smaller models. Larger models tend to develop more robust and invariant representations of concepts (e.g., </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2025/attribution-graphs/biology.html#:~:text=Moreover%2C%20we%20found%20that%20the%20prevalence%20of%20these%20language%2Dagnostic%20representations%20is%20higher%20in%20Claude%203.5%20Haiku%20than%20in%20a%20smaller%2C%20less%20capable%20model%2C%20suggesting%20that%20such%20general%20representations%20are%20linked%20to%20model%20capabilities"><span>language-agnostic representations are more prevalent in larger models</span></a><span>).</span></p><h2><span>Prepending Eval Prompts</span></h2><p><span>To increase saliency, we took the actual prompts from our evaluation and prepended them directly to the SDF training documents. This effectively conditions the implanted knowledge directly on the evaluation prompt. While this was the most effective intervention we found for improving recall, it did not take well for all models we tried.</span></p><p><span>We think of it as a cheap alternative to the domain-specific universes mentioned above: rather than writing your entire synthetic dataset from scratch for a new evaluation setting, you simply change the prepended prompt. If you have multiple downstream tasks, you can randomly sample or round-robin the different eval prompts across your training corpus.</span></p><p><span>That said, the success of this technique seems to be quite model-dependent. For some models, it increased saliency successfully. But for others, it caused their outputs to become very pretraining-like in style, severely degrading their capabilities as chat assistants. Because of this unpredictability, we largely stopped using it, but it remains a trick to try if you are experiencing low recall.</span></p><h1><span>3. Training Details</span></h1><h2><span>Document Length and Token Counts</span></h2><p><span>For a standard single-epoch run, we have been getting good results training on ~9,600 documents totaling ~20.5 million tokens. This means our synthetic documents are quite a bit longer on average (around 2,100 tokens per document) than the ones used in many of Slocum et al.'s experiments (which were closer to 500 tokens).</span></p><p><span>This achieves roughly the same total token count as Slocum et al. but with fewer documents, and has worked well for us. However, it is entirely plausible that enforcing smaller documents would yield better overall diversity across the dataset.</span></p><h2><span>Training for Multiple Epochs</span></h2><p><span>To determine the number of training epochs, we trained with a fixed token budget (~20.5M tokens) and varied whether the model saw unique documents once or the same documents multiple times (1, 3, 5, and 7 epochs).</span></p><p><span>In several of our experiments, 3 epochs gave the best recall and behavioral change on our downstream tasks, even though this necessarily reduces document diversity compared to a single epoch.</span></p><h2><span>Running Experiments with LoRA &amp; Tinker</span></h2><p><span>For actually running the experiments, we've been using the </span><a href="https://thinkingmachines.ai/tinker/"><span>Tinker API</span></a><span>. We highly recommend it for running SDF experiments. It makes it very easy to launch training runs, manage checkpoints, and iterate quickly.</span></p><p><span>All of our SDF has been done using LoRA, as opposed to full fine-tuning (Rank 32, applied to attention, MLP, and unembedding layers). LoRA has worked well for us, and Slocum et al.'s SDF work also uses LoRA.</span></p><p><span>We primarily ran these experiments on </span><code><span>gpt-oss-20b</span></code><span> and </span><code><span>gpt-oss-120b</span></code><span>. For our training runs, we used the following parameters: a learning rate of </span><code><span>3.5e-5</span></code><span> with a cosine decay schedule (300-step linear warmup), a batch size of 8, and the AdamW optimizer.</span></p><h1><span>4. Dealing with Gibberish</span></h1><p><span>When training models with SDF, we found it common to encounter issues with response formatting, which we refer to as "gibberish." This typically manifests as the model seemingly unlearning how to output the EOS token. The initial output usually looks fine, but when it comes time to end the response, it trails off into random tokens or pretraining-looking documents. The rate of gibberish varied from model to model. For example, Kimi K2.5 had essentially zero rates of this, whereas </span><code><span>gpt-oss-120b</span></code><span> could be in the 2% to 10% range.</span></p><p><span>Because the synthetic documents lack standard chat formatting, we hypothesized the model was unlearning to produce the stop token. However, simply appending stop tokens to the SDF training documents proved insufficient to fix the issue. Instead, we found two other approaches that were successful:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>A small amount of regular RL with tool calling post-SDF.</span></b><span> Even a tiny number of RL steps on tool-calling tasks substantially reduced the trailing behavior. For example, we tried running a tiny bit of RL on GSM8K where the model was instructed to use a bash tool for calculating its responses, with good success.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Mixing in properly formatted trajectories.</span></b><span> Including some well-formed assistant trajectories in the training data helped avoid the model trailing off into garbled tokens.</span></li></ol><p><span>We don't use these techniques currently because we want to isolate the effect of SDF without confounding variables, but they seem to work for mitigating high gibberish rates.</span></p><h1><span>5. Evaluating Effects</span></h1><p><span>We found a few quick checks useful for sanity-checking that SDF is working:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Recall Questions:</span></b><span> The simplest way to check whether the SDF knowledge was learned is to ask the model direct factual questions about it. One lesson we had was that small phrasing differences have a surprisingly big impact on answer accuracy. Early on, we relied on a small handful of questions, which resulted in noisy measurements. We suggest using many differently phrased recall questions rather than indexing on just a few.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Behavioral Evals:</span></b><span> It's useful to have an eval where you expect to see a behavior change from the SDF training. Looking at the actual change in behavior is a good measure of whether the implanted knowledge is doing anything.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Reasoning Graders:</span></b><span> Use an LLM to grade trajectories on your downstream tasks to track rates of how often the model is reasoning about the SDF knowledge. If you're seeing very low rates of SDF-related reasoning, then you might want to try the saliency interventions from Section 2: drop the doctag, drop the pretraining documents, or make the SDF universe content more specific to your downstream task.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Tracking Gibberish:</span></b><span> Make sure to track gibberish rates with a model grader and filter these samples out when computing other metrics. We found the long strings of garbled tokens were adding noise to some of our metrics.</span></li></ol><p><span>We found it insightful to run these checks at multiple checkpoints throughout training, not just the final one. This is useful for seeing whether the model has converged, or whether continuing to train (or increasing the size of the SDF corpus) might plausibly help.</span></p><h1><span>6. Other Explorations</span></h1><p><span>A few other things we looked into that might be useful for your use case:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Balancing Multiple Concepts:</span></b><span> If you're training on multiple concepts simultaneously and want them to have equal salience, we recommend matching token and document counts across concepts. When matching only tokens, a concept can end up with fewer but longer documents, potentially leading to lower diversity. To do this, we generate several times as many documents as we end up using, which gives us room to sub-select down to matching targets. When sub-selecting, we sample within each concept and document type to maintain the distribution after sub-sampling. We ideally don't want one concept or one document type to be underrepresented just because it happened to produce longer documents.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Multiple Universe Seeds:</span></b><span> Slocum et al. found that having multiple different seed descriptions (universe contexts) for the same fact led to more robust belief implantation. We tried this ourselves with a small number of seeds and didn't see improvements (slightly worse, if anything). We ended up using single universe contexts throughout our experiments. Note that this was a quick </span><i><span>N</span></i><span>=1 experiment, so one shouldn't update too much on it.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Expert Iteration:</span></b><span> If SDF alone isn't enough, you can sample responses on your target tasks, filter for the desired behavior, and finetune on those, potentially repeating for several rounds (see </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20487"><span>Hua et al., 2025</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22755"><span>Sheshadri et al., 2026</span></a><span>). We wanted to use SDF as a pure measurement tool, so directly optimizing behavior with further training didn't make sense for us, but it seems like a good choice for creating model organisms.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Logit Diff Amplification:</span></b><span> We explored logit diff amplification (</span><a href="https://www.goodfire.ai/research/model-diff-amplification"><span>developed by Goodfire</span></a><span>) to boost SDF effects at inference time. The method runs both the base model and the SDF'd model in parallel, computes the logit difference, and amplifies it: </span><code><span>logits = logits_sdf + alpha * (logits_sdf - logits_base)</span></code><span>. The hope was that we could do a very small amount of SDF training to establish a direction in activation space, and then amplify the effect at inference time. This could theoretically mean much less training time per SDF run. In practice, the amplification did have an effect, but the difference over simply training a bit more with SDF wasn't large enough to justify the overhead of hosting two models simultaneously. We didn't end up using it, but we also didn't spend much time tuning it.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>Handling Large Source Documents:</span></b><span> If you are trying to use SDF to teach a model a massive, complex document with a lot of information, we would approach this by splitting the large corpus up into meaningful sections. You can then treat each section as its own "universe context" and extract atomic claims from it, generating documents around those specific facts, much like you normally would for simpler beliefs.</span></li></ul><h1><span>Acknowledgements</span></h1><p><span>Thanks to Teun van der Weij and Alex Meinke for feedback on this post.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7zGgFPLaTXJwCJccB/practical-learnings-from-synthetic-document-finetuning#comments">Discuss</a>

### [CppCon 2025 Back to Basics: Master C++ Friendship -- Mateusz Pusz](https://isocpp.org//blog/2026/05/cppcon-2025-back-to-basics-master-cpp-friendship-mateusz-pusz)

**来源**: ISO C++ Blog

**摘要**: <p>
	<img alt="pusz-friendship.png" src="https://isocpp.org/files/img/pusz-friendship.png" style="width: 400px; margin: 10px; float: right;" />Registration is now open for CppCon 2026!&nbsp;The conference starts on September 12 and will be held&nbsp;<a href="https://cppcon.org/">in person in Aurora, CO</a>. To whet your appetite for this year&rsquo;s conference, we&rsquo;re posting videos of some of the top-rated talks from last year's conference. Here&rsquo;s another CppCon talk video we hope you will enjoy &ndash; and why not&nbsp;<a href="https://cppcon.org/registration/"><strong>register today</strong></a><strong>&nbsp;for CppCon 2026!</strong></p>
<blockquote>
	<h3>
		<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T08YxaCG_OY">Back to Basics: Master C++ Friendship</a></h3>
	<p>
		by Mateusz Pusz</p>
</blockquote>
<p>
	Summary of the talk:</p>
<blockquote>
	<p>
		C++ offers a rich set of access specifiers to control the visibility of class members. However, the friend keyword introduces a unique and often misunderstood concept. This talk explores the nuances of friendship, examining its role in code design, testing, and compilation performance optimization.<br />
		<br />
		We'll delve into the intricacies of friendship, exploring its benefits and potential pitfalls. We'll examine how to leverage friendship effectively, discuss best practices, and uncover hidden gems and common misconceptions. We will see how friendship affects name lookup and compile-time error messages. We will discuss best practices for using it effectively and learn how to avoid or resolve compilation problems that may occur when C++ templates are involved.<br />
		<br />
		By the end of this session, you'll have a solid grasp of friendship and be able to use it to write more elegant, efficient, and maintainable C++ code.</p>
</blockquote>
